=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string source of size n, a string pattern that is a subsequence of source, and a sorted integer array targetIndices that contains distinct numbers in the range [0, n - 1].
We define an operation as removing a character at an index idx from source such that:

idx is an element of targetIndices.
pattern remains a subsequence of source after removing the character.

Performing an operation does not change the indices of the other characters in source. For example, if you remove 'c' from "acb", the character at index 2 would still be 'b'.
Return the maximum number of operations that can be performed.
 
Example 1:

Input: source = "abbaa", pattern = "aba", targetIndices = [0,1,2]
Output: 1
Explanation:
We can't remove source[0] but we can do either of these two operations:

Remove source[1], so that source becomes "a_baa".
Remove source[2], so that source becomes "ab_aa".


Example 2:

Input: source = "bcda", pattern = "d", targetIndices = [0,3]
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can remove source[0] and source[3] in two operations.

Example 3:

Input: source = "dda", pattern = "dda", targetIndices = [0,1,2]
Output: 0
Explanation:
We can't remove any character from source.

Example 4:

Input: source = "yeyeykyded", pattern = "yeyyd", targetIndices = [0,2,3,4]
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can remove source[2] and source[3] in two operations.

 
Constraints:

1 <= n == source.length <= 3 * 10^3
1 <= pattern.length <= n
1 <= targetIndices.length <= n
targetIndices is sorted in ascending order.
The input is generated such that targetIndices contains distinct elements in the range [0, n - 1].
source and pattern consist only of lowercase English letters.
The input is generated such that pattern appears as a subsequence in source.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's ensure code syntax:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)
        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True
        
        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0
        
        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            # update descending
            # local variables for speed
            # We can loop j from m down to 1
            for j in range(m, 0, -1):
                if pattern[j-1] == ch:
                    val = dp[j-1] + cost
                    if val < dp[j]:
                        dp[j] = val
        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]

When processing j, dp[j] might have been updated for a larger pattern index? No, dp[j] is for current j; not updated earlier because each j processed once. It remains previous dp[j] (unless j was updated as dp[j-1] for j+1? Wait when processing j+1, we update dp[j+1], not dp[j]. So dp[j] unchanged. Good. When processing j, dp[j-1] previous because not processed yet. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a way to compute min cost using LCS-like DP with target weights. Our DP is standard.

Let's formalize DP invariant:
After processing first i characters of source, dp[j] = minimum target positions used among subsequences of source[:i] equal to pattern[:j]. dp[0]=0. When considering source[i], for each j where pattern[j-1]==source[i], we can append source[i] to a match of pattern[:j-1], cost dp[j-1]+is_target[i]; or skip source[i], dp[j] remains. Descending ensures each source char used at most once. At end dp[m] min.

Now, let's ensure final code block includes only code, no explanation. The final answer can be:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)
        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            for j in range(m, 0, -1):
                if pattern[j - 1] == ch:
                    new_val = dp[j - 1] + cost
                    if new_val < dp[j]:
                        dp[j] = new_val

        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]
```

for i, ch in enumerate(source):
    cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
    for j in range(m, 0, -1):
        if p[j-1] == ch:
            cand = dp[j-1] + cost
            if cand < dp[j]:
                dp[j] = cand

Now, let's maybe derive a proof to reassure:
Let f(E) = number of target positions in embedding E. For any feasible deletion set R, there exists embedding E in remaining source. Since E positions are not deleted, E∩T ⊆ T\R, so |R| ≤ K - |E∩T| ≤ K - min_E f(E). Conversely, choose E minimizing f. Delete all target positions not in E. Then E remains, so pattern is subsequence. Number deletions = K - f(E). Thus maximum = K - min f. DP computes min f.

This is solid.

=== ANSWER ===
to the user's request with the final Python code.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)

        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            for j in range(m, 0, -1):
                if pattern[j - 1] == ch:
                    cand = dp[j - 1] + cost
                    if cand < dp[j]:
                        dp[j] = cand

        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]
```
